\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(uv^{N+1}\right)^{2N+4}x_{j}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(u^{N}w\right)^{2N+4}x_{j}^{-1}\right)\times

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{-\frac{1}{2}}u^{2(N+2)^{2}}x_{j}q^{-1-k_{j}}\right)}{\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{-\frac{1}{2}}u^{2(N+2)^{2}}x_{j}q^{-1-k_{j}+k_{N+2}}\right)}\frac{\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}u^{-2(N+2)^{2}}x_{j}^{-1}q^{1-k_{N+2}}\right)}{\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}u^{-2(N+2)^{2}}x_{j}^{-1}q^{1-k_{N+2}+k_{j}}\right)}\times

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}\left(q^{k_{N+2}-1}\left(uv^{-1}\right)^{2(N+1)(N+2)}\right)\left[\prod\limits_{i\neq j}^{N+1}\frac{\Gamma_{e}\left(\frac{x_{i}}{x_{j}}q^{-k_{i}}\right)}{\Gamma_{e}\left(\frac{x_{i}}{x_{j}}q^{k_{j}-k_{i}}\right)}\right]\prod\limits_{i=1}^{N+1}\Gamma_{e}\left(x_{i}\tilde{x}_{j}^{-1}q^{-k_{i}}\right)\times

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}u^{-2(N+2)^{2}}\tilde{x}_{j}^{-1}q^{1-k_{N+2}}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(u^{N}w^{2}\right)^{-2N-4}x_{j}q^{1-k_{j}}\right)\times

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}u^{-N-3}v^{N+1}w^{2}a_{l}^{-1}x_{j}^{-1}q^{k_{j}}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left(pq^{3-k_{N+2}}\left(u^{N+1}w\right)^{-4N-8}\right)\times

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(uv^{N+1}\right)^{-2N-4}x_{j}^{-1}q^{k_{j}}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}u^{2(N+2)^{2}}x_{j}q^{-k_{j}}\right)\,,

(B.26)

where
\vec{k}
labels a partitions of 1,
i.e.
it is the vector with only one of the entries equal to one and all others are zero.
Now we S glue this tube to an arbitrary theory with
\mathrm{SU}(N+1)_{\tilde{x}}
global symmetry in a usual way

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}^{\vec{k}}_{1}(x)=\kappa_{N}\oint\prod\limits_{i=1}^{N}\frac{d\tilde{x}_{i}}{2\pi i\tilde{x}_{i}}\prod\limits_{i\neq j}^{N+1}\frac{1}{\Gamma_{e}\left(\frac{\tilde{x}_{i}}{\tilde{x}_{j}}\right)}K_{(4;\vec{k})}^{A_{N}}(\tilde{x},x){\mathcal{I}}_{0}(\tilde{x})\,,

(B.27)

As usually there are contour pinchings that depend on a particular choice of the partition
\vec{k}
in the tube (
B.26
).
These pinchings cancel the zero in the index of the tube
coming from the term
\Gamma_{e}\left(pq^{2-k_{N+2}}\right)
. Just like in the previous section there are two distinct classes of partitions contributing.

I.
First class corresponds to the following partition
\vec{k}_{i}

\displaystyle k_{i}=1\,,1\leq i\leq N+1\,,\quad k_{j}=0~~\forall~~j\neq i\,.

(B.28)

For this partition tube index (
B.26
) reduces to

\displaystyle K_{(4;\vec{k}_{i})}(\tilde{x},x)=\Gamma_{e}\left(pq^{2}\right)C_{i}\prod\limits_{l=1}^{2N+4}\prod\limits_{j\neq i}^{N+1}\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(u^{N}w^{2}\right)^{-2N-4}x_{j}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}u^{-2(N+2)^{2}}\tilde{x}_{j}^{-1}q\right)\times

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(u^{N}w^{2}\right)^{-2N-4}x_{j}q\right)\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}u^{2(N+2)^{2}}x_{j}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(uv^{N+1}\right)^{2N+4}x_{i}\right)\times

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}u^{N+3}v^{-N-1}w^{-2}a_{l}x_{i}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}u^{-2(N+2)^{2}}\tilde{x}_{i}^{-1}q\right)\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}u^{2(N+2)^{2}}x_{i}q^{-1}\right)\times

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}u^{-N-3}v^{N+1}w^{2}x_{i}^{-1}a_{l}^{-1}q\right)\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(uv^{N+1}\right)^{-2N-4}x_{i}^{-1}q\right)\theta_{p}\left(\frac{x_{j}}{x_{i}}\right)^{-1}\times

\displaystyle\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}u^{-2(N+2)^{2}}x_{i}^{-1}q\right)^{-1}\left[\prod\limits_{m=1}^{N+1}\Gamma_{e}\left(x_{j}\tilde{x}_{m}^{-1}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left(q^{-1}x_{i}\tilde{x}_{m}^{-1}\right)\right]\,,

where

\displaystyle C_{i}=\Gamma_{e}\left(q^{-1}\left(uv^{-1}\right)^{2(N+1)(N+2)}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left(q^{-1}u^{(N+1)(2N+5)}v^{N+1}w^{2}a_{l}^{-1}\right)\times

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}\left(pq^{3}\left(u^{N+1}w\right)^{-4N-8}\right)\,.

(B.30)

Once we glue this tube to an arbitrary theory we obtain contour pinching at

\displaystyle\tilde{x}_{j}=q^{k_{j}}x_{\sigma(j)}\,,\qquad\tilde{x}_{i}=q^{k_{i}-1}x_{\sigma(i)}\,,\quad\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N+1}k_{i}=1\,.

(B.31)

where
\sigma(i)
are permutations. We fix these permutations to
\sigma(i)=i
. All other permutations give the same result so
we can just multiply the answer with the irrelevant constant factor of
(N+1)!
. Once again we have two distinct classes of
partitions of
1
.

I.a.
First partition is
k_{i}=1\,,k_{j}=0~~\forall~~j\neq i
which corresponds to the pinching at
\tilde{x}_{j}=x_{j}

\forall~~j
. In this case we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{1}^{(\vec{k}_{i};x)}(x)=\prod\limits_{j\neq i}^{N+1}\frac{\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(u^{N}w^{2}\right)^{-2N-4}x_{j}\right)\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}u^{-2(N+2)^{2}}x_{j}^{-1}\right)}{\theta_{p}\left(\frac{x_{j}}{x_{i}}\right)\theta_{p}\left(q^{-1}\frac{x_{i}}{x_{j}}\right)}\times

\displaystyle\frac{\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(uv^{N+1}\right)^{-2N-4}x_{i}^{-1}\right)}{\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}u^{-2(N+2)^{2}}qx_{i}^{-1}\right)}\prod\limits_{l=1}^{2N+4}\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}u^{-N-3}v^{N+1}w^{2}a_{l}^{-1}x_{i}^{-1}\right)C_{i}{\mathcal{I}}_{0}(x)\,,

(B.32)

I.b.
Second class of possible partitions is
k_{m}=1
for some
m\neq i
and all other
k_{j}=0
including
j=i
. This choice of the partition
corresponds to the following pinching:

\displaystyle\tilde{x}_{i}=q^{-1}x_{i}\,,\quad\tilde{x}_{m}=qx_{m}\,,\quad\tilde{x}_{j}=x_{j}\,,~\forall~j\neq i\neq m\,.

(B.33)

In this case we obtain:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{1}^{(\vec{k}_{i};qx)}(x)=\sum\limits_{m\neq i}\frac{\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(u^{N}w^{2}\right)^{-2N-4}x_{m}\right)\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(uv^{N+1}\right)^{-2N-4}x_{i}^{-1}\right)}{\theta_{p}\left(\frac{x_{m}}{x_{i}}\right)\theta_{p}\left(q\frac{x_{m}}{x_{i}}\right)}\times

\displaystyle\prod\limits_{l=1}^{2N+4}\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}u^{-N-3}v^{N+1}w^{2}a_{l}^{-1}x_{i}^{-1}\right)\times

\displaystyle\prod\limits_{j\neq m\neq i}^{N+1}\frac{\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}u^{-2(N+2)^{2}}x_{j}^{-1}\right)\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(u^{N}w^{2}\right)^{-2N-4}x_{j}\right)}{\theta_{p}\left(\frac{x_{j}}{x_{i}}\right)\theta_{p}\left(\frac{x_{m}}{x_{j}}\right)}\Delta_{im}^{(1,0)}C_{i}{\mathcal{I}}_{0}(x)\,.

(B.34)

II.
Second class of pinchings in the gluing (
B.27
) corresponds to the partition:

\displaystyle k_{N+2}=1\,,\quad k_{i}=0\,.

(B.35)

This partition leads to the following index of the tube theory:

\displaystyle K_{(4;k_{N+2})}^{A_{N}}(x,\tilde{x})=\Gamma_{e}\left(pq\right)C_{N+2}\prod\limits_{j=1}^{N+1}\prod\limits_{l=1}^{2N+4}\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(u^{N}w^{2}\right)^{2N+4}x_{j}^{-1}\right)\times

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}u^{-2(N+2)^{2}}\tilde{x}_{j}^{-1}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(u^{N}w^{2}\right)^{-2N-4}x_{j}q\right)\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}u^{2(N+2)^{2}}x_{j}\right)\times

\displaystyle\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{-\frac{1}{2}}u^{2(N+2)^{2}}x_{j}q^{-1}\right)^{-1}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{N+1}\Gamma_{e}\left(x_{i}\tilde{x}_{j}^{-1}\right)\,,

(B.36)

where

\displaystyle C_{N+2}=\Gamma_{e}\left(\left(uv^{-1}\right)^{2(N+1)(N+2)}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left(u^{(N+1)(2N+5)}v^{N+1}w^{2}a_{l}^{-1}\right)\times

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}\left(pq^{2}\left(u^{N+1}w\right)^{-4N-8}\right)\,.

(B.37)

Once we glue it to the index of the arbitrary theory we obtain the pinching at
\tilde{x}_{i}=x_{i}
resulting in

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}^{k_{N+2}}(x)=C_{N+2}\prod\limits_{j=1}^{N+1}\frac{\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(u^{N}w^{2}\right)^{-2N-4}x_{j}\right)}{\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{-\frac{1}{2}}u^{2(N+2)^{2}}x_{j}q^{-1}\right)}{\mathcal{I}}_{0}(x)\,.

(B.38)

Finally one should perform similar computation for the pinching specified in the second line of (
B.25
). This
calculation is basically identical and leads to exactly the same results. We leave the calculation to the reader.
Summing terms (
B.32
), (
B.34
) and (
B.38
) and performing
conjugation (
4.11
) we finally obtain the A
\Delta
O (
4.15
) with the
shift term given in (
4.16
) and constant term of (
4.17
).

B.4
Difference operator from
A_{1}^{1}
trinion.

In the present appendix we give details of the derivation of the A
\Delta
O (
3.28
) otained using
A_{1}^{1}
trinion theory.
We start with computing
indices of the tube theories (
3.27
),(
3.26
) with and without the defects. For this we start with the trinon index
(
3.24
) and set
\epsilon=(pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{2}a^{2}q
or
\epsilon=(pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{2}a^{2}
. Let’s consider the case of closing
without the defect. In this case substituting the value above we obtain

\displaystyle K^{A_{1}^{1}}_{(2;0)}(v,z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa^{2}\oint\frac{dy_{1}}{4\pi iy_{1}}\oint\frac{dy_{2}}{4\pi iy_{2}}\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{4}\Gamma_{e}\left(t^{-1}a^{-1}c_{i}y_{1}^{\pm 1}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left(t^{-1}a^{-1}\widetilde{c}_{i}y_{2}^{\pm 1}\right)}{\Gamma_{e}\left(y_{1}^{\pm 2}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left(y_{2}^{\pm 2}\right)}

(B.39)

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{3}{\Gamma_{e}\left(pqt^{2}a^{2}c_{i}c_{4}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left(pqt^{2}a^{2}\widetilde{c}_{i}\widetilde{c}_{4}\right)}\,\Gamma_{e}(a^{-4})

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}\left(\left(pq\right)^{1/2}a^{2}y_{1}^{\pm 1}z^{\pm 1}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left(\left(pq\right)^{1/2}z^{\pm 1}y_{2}^{\pm 1}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left(t^{-2}a^{-2}z^{\pm 1}v^{\pm 1}\right)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}\left(\left(pq\right)^{1/2}t^{2}y_{1}^{\pm 1}v^{\pm 1}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left(\left(pq\right)^{1/2}t^{2}a^{2}v^{\pm 1}y_{2}^{\pm 1}\right)\,.

Now we can use the duality identity (
A.10
). We split variables so that
t^{-1}a^{-1}\widetilde{c}_{i}
is in the first group of four
and
(pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}z^{\pm 1}
with
(pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{2}a^{2}v^{\pm 1}
in the second group of four. We then obtain:

\displaystyle K^{A_{1}^{1}}_{(2;0)}(v,z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa^{2}\Gamma_{e}\left(pq\right)\oint\frac{dy_{1}}{4\pi iy_{1}}\oint\frac{dy_{2}}{4\pi iy_{2}}\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{4}\Gamma_{e}\left(t^{-1}a^{-1}c_{i}y_{1}^{\pm 1}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}ta\widetilde{c}_{i}y_{2}^{\pm 1}\right)}{\Gamma_{e}\left(y_{1}^{\pm 2}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left(y_{2}^{\pm 2}\right)}

(B.40)

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{3}{\Gamma_{e}\left(pqt^{2}a^{2}c_{i}c_{4}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left(t^{-2}a^{-2}\widetilde{c}_{i}\widetilde{c}_{4}\right)}\,\Gamma_{e}(a^{-4})\Gamma_{e}(pqt^{4}a^{4})\Gamma_{e}\left(v^{\pm 1}y_{2}^{\pm 1}\right)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}\left(\left(pq\right)^{1/2}a^{2}y_{1}^{\pm 1}z^{\pm 1}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left(t^{-2}a^{-2}z^{\pm 1}y_{2}^{\pm 1}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left(\left(pq\right)^{1/2}t^{2}y_{1}^{\pm 1}v^{\pm 1}\right)\,.

Notice that there is
\Gamma_{e}\left(pq\right)
factor which equals zero. However, as usual, at the same time there is pinching of the integration contour taking place at
y_{1}=v^{\pm 1}
which cancels
this zero. Computing contribution of this pinching we finally obtain an expression (
3.26
). In an identical
way we can obtain the tube (
3.27
) with the defect denoted as
K^{A_{1}^{1}}_{(2;1)}(v,z)
.

Now having expressions for both tubes we can conjugate one of them and perform S gluings of the following form:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}\,\cdot\,{\mathcal{I}}(v)=\kappa^{2}\oint\frac{dz}{4\pi iz}\frac{dw}{4\pi iw}\frac{1}{\Gamma_{e}\left(z^{\pm 2}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left(w^{\pm 2}\right)}K^{A_{1}^{1}}_{(2;1)}(v,z)\bar{K}^{A_{1}^{1}}_{(2;0)}(w,z){\mathcal{I}}(w)\,,

(B.41)

where
\bar{K}^{A_{1}^{1}}_{(2,0)}
is the conjugated tube,
i.e.
the one with all of the charges flipped. Substituting (
3.26
) and (
3.27
)
into expression above we obtain the following result:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{I}(v)=\kappa^{4}\oint\frac{dw}{4\pi iw}\frac{1}{\Gamma_{e}(w^{\pm 2})}\oint\frac{dz}{4\pi iz}\frac{1}{\Gamma_{e}(z^{\pm 2})}\oint\frac{dy_{1}}{4\pi iy_{1}}\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{4}\Gamma_{e}\left(q^{-\frac{1}{2}}t^{-1}a^{-1}c_{i}y_{1}^{\pm 1}\right)}{\Gamma_{e}\left(y_{1}^{\pm 2}\right)}

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}\left(\left(pq\right)^{1/2}q^{\frac{1}{2}}a^{2}y_{1}^{\pm 1}z^{\pm 1}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left(\left(pq\right)^{1/2}q^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{2}y_{1}^{\pm 1}v^{\pm 1}\right)\Bigg[\frac{\Gamma_{e}(v^{2})}{\Gamma_{e}(qv^{2})}\Gamma_{e}\left(t^{-2}a^{-2}vz^{\pm 1}\right)\times

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}\left(q^{-1}t^{-2}a^{-2}v^{-1}z^{\pm 1}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}qta\widetilde{c}_{i}v\right)\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}ta\widetilde{c}_{i}v^{-1}\right)+\{v\leftrightarrow v^{-1}\}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\oint\frac{dx_{1}}{4\pi ix_{1}}\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{4}\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{-1}a^{-1}c_{i}^{-1}x_{1}^{\pm 1}\right)}{\Gamma_{e}\left(x_{1}^{\pm 2}\right)}\Gamma_{e}\left(t^{2}x_{1}^{\pm 1}z^{\pm 1}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left(a^{2}x_{1}^{\pm 1}w^{\pm 1}\right)\times

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{-1}a^{-1}\widetilde{c}_{i}^{-1}w^{\pm 1}\right)\mathcal{I}(w)\,.

(B.42)

Next we perform two duality transformations of (
A.10
) type.
First one is on
x_{1}
node. The split of the fields is chosen so that those charged under
z
or
w
are grouped together
and so the gauge singlets of the dual theory contain a bifundamental of
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{z}
and
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{w}
gauge groups. Second duality is performed on the
z
node.
Similarly to the first duality the fields charged under
w
or
v
are grouped together. We then get,

\displaystyle\kappa^{4}\frac{\Gamma_{e}(v^{2})}{\Gamma_{e}(qv^{2})}\oint\frac{dw}{4\pi iw}\frac{1}{\Gamma_{e}(w^{\pm 2})}\oint\frac{dz}{4\pi iz}\frac{1}{\Gamma_{e}(z^{\pm 2})}\oint\frac{dy_{1}}{4\pi iy_{1}}\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{4}\Gamma_{e}\left(q^{-\frac{1}{2}}t^{-1}a^{-1}c_{i}y_{1}^{\pm 1}\right)}{\Gamma_{e}\left(y_{1}^{\pm 2}\right)}\times

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}\left(a^{2}y_{1}^{\pm 1}z^{\pm 1}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left(\left(pq\right)^{1/2}q^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{2}y_{1}^{\pm 1}v^{\pm 1}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}q^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{-2}a^{-2}vz^{\pm 1}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}qta\widetilde{c}_{i}v\right)\times

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}q^{-\frac{1}{2}}t^{-2}a^{-2}v^{-1}z^{\pm 1}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}ta\widetilde{c}_{i}v^{-1}\right)\oint\frac{dx_{1}}{4\pi ix_{1}}\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{4}\Gamma_{e}\left(tac_{i}^{-1}x_{1}^{\pm 1}\right)}{\Gamma_{e}\left(x_{1}^{\pm 2}\right)}\times

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}\left(q^{-\frac{1}{2}}a^{-2}x_{1}^{\pm 1}z^{\pm 1}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{-2}x_{1}^{\pm 1}w^{\pm 1}\right)\Gamma_{e}((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}q^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{2}a^{2}z^{\pm 1}w^{\pm 1})\Gamma_{e}(vw^{\pm 1})\times

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}(q^{-1}v^{-1}w^{\pm 1})\Gamma_{e}(pqq^{\frac{1}{2}}x_{1}^{\pm 1}y_{1}^{\pm 1})\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{-1}a^{-1}\widetilde{c}_{i}^{-1}w^{\pm 1}\right)\mathcal{T}(w)+\{v\leftrightarrow v^{-1}\}

(B.43)

The poles of
\Gamma_{e}\left(q^{-1}v^{-1}w^{\pm 1}\right)
and
\Gamma_{e}\left(vw^{\pm 1}\right)
collide when
w=v^{\pm 1}
or
w=(qv)^{\pm 1}
which results in the integration contour
pinching. We should sum over contributions of all these pinchings. Terms with opposite powers contribute equivalently since
w
is a fugacity for the Cartan generator of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
.
Substituting values of
w
at the pinching points we can integrate out first
z
and then
x_{1}
integrals using
A_{1}
elliptic beta integral (
A.4
).
The result of these integrations is

\displaystyle\kappa\Gamma_{e}(a^{4})\Gamma_{e}(q^{-1}a^{-4})\oint\frac{dy_{1}}{4\pi iy_{1}}\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{4}\Gamma_{e}\left(q^{-\frac{1}{2}}t^{-1}a^{-1}c_{i}y_{1}^{\pm 1}\right)}{\Gamma_{e}\left(y_{1}^{\pm 2}\right)}\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}q^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{-2}vy_{1}^{\pm 1}\right)\times

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}q^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{2}a^{4}v^{-1}y_{1}^{\pm 1}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left(\left(pq\right)^{1/2}q^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{2}y_{1}^{\pm 1}v^{\pm 1}\right)\frac{\Gamma_{e}(v^{2})\Gamma_{e}(q^{-1}v^{-2})}{\Gamma_{e}(qv^{2})\Gamma_{e}(v^{-2})}\times

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{4}\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}qta\widetilde{c}_{i}v\right)\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{-1}a^{-1}\widetilde{c}_{i}^{-1}v^{-1}\right)\Gamma_{e}(pqt^{-4}a^{-4})\prod_{i=1}^{4}\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{-1}a^{-3}c_{i}^{-1}v\right)\times

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{-1}ac_{i}^{-1}v^{-1}\right)\prod_{j<k}\Gamma_{e}(t^{2}a^{2}c_{j}^{-1}c_{k}^{-1})\mathcal{I}(v)+\{v\leftrightarrow v^{-1}\}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\kappa\Gamma_{e}(pqt^{-4}a^{-4})\Gamma_{e}(a^{4})\Gamma_{e}(q^{-1}a^{-4})\oint\frac{dy_{1}}{4\pi iy_{1}}\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{4}\Gamma_{e}\left(q^{-\frac{1}{2}}t^{-1}a^{-1}c_{i}y_{1}^{\pm 1}\right)}{\Gamma_{e}\left(y_{1}^{\pm 2}\right)}\times

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}q^{-\frac{1}{2}}t^{-2}v^{-1}y_{1}^{\pm 1}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}q^{\frac{3}{2}}t^{2}a^{4}vy_{1}^{\pm 1}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left(\left(pq\right)^{1/2}q^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{2}y_{1}^{\pm 1}v^{\pm 1}\right)\frac{\Gamma_{e}(v^{2})}{\Gamma_{e}(q^{2}v^{2})}\times

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{4}\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}qta\widetilde{c}_{i}v\right)\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}ta\widetilde{c}_{i}v^{-1}\right)\prod_{j<k}\Gamma_{e}(t^{2}a^{2}c_{j}^{-1}c_{k}^{-1})\prod_{i=1}^{4}\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}q^{-1}t^{-1}a^{-3}c_{i}^{-1}v^{-1}\right)\times

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}qt^{-1}ac_{i}^{-1}v\right)\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{-1}a^{-1}\widetilde{c}_{i}^{-1}(qv)^{\pm 1}\right)\mathcal{I}(qv)+\{v\leftrightarrow v^{-1}\}\,,

(B.44)

where the first term comes from the pinching at
w=v^{\pm 1}
and the second from the pinching at
w=(qv)^{\pm 1}
.
In both of the terms above we get an
\mathrm{SU}(2)
theory with
4
flavors. In order to proceed we perform (
A.10
) duality
for these two theories. For this in each of the cases we group together fields transforming in the fundamental representation of

\mathrm{SU}(4)_{c}
global symmetry. Dual expressions are then given by

\displaystyle\kappa\Gamma_{e}(a^{4})\Gamma_{e}(q^{-1}a^{-4})\Gamma_{e}(pq^{2})\Gamma_{e}(pq^{2}a^{4})\Gamma_{e}(pq^{2}t^{4}a^{4})\Gamma_{e}(pq^{2}t^{4})\Gamma_{e}(pq^{2}v^{2})\Gamma_{e}(pq^{2}t^{4}a^{4}v^{-2})\times

\displaystyle\prod_{j<k}\Gamma_{e}(q^{-1}t^{-2}a^{-2}c_{j}c_{k})\oint\frac{dy_{1}}{4\pi iy_{1}}\frac{1}{\Gamma_{e}(y_{1}^{\pm 2})}\prod_{i=1}^{4}\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}q^{\frac{1}{2}}tac_{i}y_{1}^{\pm 1}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left(q^{-\frac{1}{2}}t^{-4}a^{-2}vy_{1}^{\pm 1}\right)\times

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}\left(q^{-\frac{1}{2}}a^{2}v^{-1}y_{1}^{\pm 1}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left(q^{-\frac{1}{2}}a^{-2}v^{\pm 1}y_{1}^{\pm 1}\right)\frac{\Gamma_{e}(v^{2})\Gamma_{e}(q^{-1}v^{-2})}{\Gamma_{e}(qv^{2})\Gamma_{e}(v^{-2})}\prod_{i=1}^{4}\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}qta\widetilde{c}_{i}v\right)\times

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{-1}a^{-1}\widetilde{c}_{i}^{-1}v^{-1}\right)\Gamma_{e}(pqt^{-4}a^{-4})\prod_{i=1}^{4}\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{-1}a^{-3}c_{i}^{-1}v\right)\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{-1}ac_{i}^{-1}v^{-1}\right)\times

\displaystyle\prod_{j<k}\Gamma_{e}(t^{2}a^{2}c_{j}^{-1}c_{k}^{-1})\mathcal{I}(v)+\{v\leftrightarrow v^{-1}\}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\kappa\Gamma_{e}(pq)\Gamma_{e}(pqv^{-2})\Gamma_{e}(pq^{2}a^{4})\Gamma_{e}(pq^{3}t^{4}a^{4})\Gamma_{e}(pq^{3}t^{4}a^{4}v^{2})\Gamma_{e}(pq^{2}t^{4})\Gamma_{e}(pqt^{-4}a^{-4})\Gamma_{e}(a^{4})\Gamma_{e}(q^{-1}a^{-4})\times

\displaystyle\oint\frac{dy_{1}}{4\pi iy_{1}}\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{4}\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}q^{\frac{1}{2}}tac_{i}y_{1}^{\pm 1}\right)}{\Gamma_{e}\left(y_{1}^{\pm 2}\right)}\Gamma_{e}\left(q^{-\frac{3}{2}}t^{-4}a^{-2}v^{-1}y_{1}^{\pm 1}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left(q^{\frac{1}{2}}a^{2}vy_{1}^{\pm 1}\right)\frac{\Gamma_{e}(v^{2})}{\Gamma_{e}(q^{2}v^{2})}\times

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}\left(q^{-\frac{1}{2}}a^{-2}v^{\pm 1}y_{1}^{\pm 1}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{4}\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}qta\widetilde{c}_{i}v\right)\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}ta\widetilde{c}_{i}v^{-1}\right)\prod_{j<k}\Gamma_{e}(t^{2}a^{2}c_{j}^{-1}c_{k}^{-1})\times

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}(q^{-1}t^{-2}a^{-2}c_{j}c_{k})\prod_{i=1}^{4}\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}q^{-1}t^{-1}a^{-3}c_{i}^{-1}v^{-1}\right)\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}qt^{-1}ac_{i}^{-1}v\right)\times

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{-1}a^{-1}\widetilde{c}_{i}^{-1}(qv)^{\pm 1}\right)\mathcal{I}(qv)+\{v\leftrightarrow v^{-1}\}

(B.45)

As usually we obtain zeroes in these expressions. In particular first term contains
\Gamma_{e}\left(pq^{2}\right)
and second
\Gamma_{e}\left(pq\right)
prefactors.
These zeroes are cancelled by pinchings of integration contours. In the first term pinching takes place at

y_{1}=(q^{\frac{1}{2}}a^{2}v^{-1})^{\pm 1}
and
y_{1}=(q^{-\frac{1}{2}}a^{2}v^{-1})^{\pm 1}
. Pinching in the second term is located at
y_{1}=(q^{\frac{1}{2}}a^{2}v)^{\pm 1}
.
Substituting these values into expressions above we precisely get the operator
{\mathcal{O}}_{v}^{(\epsilon;t^{2}a^{2};1,0)}
given by (
3.28
)
and equal to the van Diejen operator (
3.1
) upon the identification (
3.29
) of its parameters.

In a completely similar way we can derive A
\Delta
Os for any pair of punctures we close and we act on. For example
the operator acting on
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{z}
puncture that is obtained by giving vev to the moment map of
\mathrm{U}(1)_{\epsilon}
puncture with the
charge
t^{2}a^{2}
is given by,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{z}^{(\epsilon;t^{2}a^{2};1,0)}\cdot{\cal I}(z)=\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{4}\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}ta^{-1}c_{i}^{-1}z\right)\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}ta\widetilde{c}_{i}^{-1}z\right)}{\theta_{p}\left(z^{2}\right)\theta_{p}\left(qz^{2}\right)}\mathcal{I}(qz)+

\displaystyle+\frac{\theta_{p}\left(q^{-1}t^{-4}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{4}\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}ta^{3}c_{i}^{-1}z^{-1}\right)\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}ta\widetilde{c}_{i}^{-1}z\right)}{\theta_{p}\left(q^{-2}t^{-4}a^{-4}\right)\theta_{p}\left(z^{2}\right)\theta_{p}\left(a^{4}z^{-2}\right)}\mathcal{I}(z)+

\displaystyle+\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{4}\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}ta^{-1}c_{i}^{-1}z\right)\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}ta\widetilde{c}_{i}^{-1}z\right)}{\theta_{p}\left(q^{-2}t^{-4}a^{-4}\right)\theta_{p}\left(z^{2}\right)\theta_{p}\left(q^{-1}z^{-2}\right)\theta_{p}\left(a^{-4}z^{2}\right)}\times

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\theta_{p}\left(q^{-1}a^{-4}\right)\theta_{p}\left(q^{-1}t^{-4}a^{-4}z^{2}\right){\mathcal{I}}(z)+\{z\leftrightarrow z^{-1}\}\,,

(B.46)

Another example is an operator acting on the
U(1)_{\epsilon}
puncture obtained by closing the
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{v}
puncture
giving vev to the moment map with the cahrge
ta\widetilde{c}_{4}
. The result is given by,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}^{(v;ta\widetilde{c}_{4};1,0)}\cdot{\cal I}(\epsilon)=\frac{\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{-2}a^{\pm 2}\epsilon\right)\prod_{j=1}^{3}\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}\widetilde{c}_{4}^{-1}\widetilde{c}_{j}^{-1}\epsilon\right)\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}c_{4}^{-1}c_{j}^{-1}\epsilon\right)}{\theta_{p}\left(\epsilon^{2}\right)\theta_{p}\left(q\epsilon^{2}\right)}\mathcal{I}(q\epsilon)+

\displaystyle\frac{\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{2}a^{2}\epsilon\right)\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{2}\widetilde{c}_{4}c_{4}^{-1}\epsilon^{-1}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{3}\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}\widetilde{c}_{4}\widetilde{c}_{j}\epsilon\right)\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}a^{2}\widetilde{c}_{4}{c}_{j}\epsilon^{-1}\right)}{\theta_{p}\left(q^{-2}t^{-2}a^{-2}\widetilde{c}_{4}^{-2}\right)\theta_{p}\left(\epsilon^{2}\right)\theta_{p}\left(a^{2}\widetilde{c}_{4}c_{4}^{-1}\epsilon^{-2}\right)}\times

\displaystyle\theta_{p}\left(q^{-1}t^{-2}\widetilde{c}_{4}^{-1}c_{4}^{-1}\right)\mathcal{I}(\epsilon)+\frac{\theta_{p}\left(q^{-1}a^{-2}\widetilde{c}_{4}^{-1}c_{4}\right)\theta_{p}\left(q^{-1}t^{-2}a^{-2}\widetilde{c}_{4}^{-2}\epsilon^{2}\right)\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{2}a^{\pm 2}\epsilon\right)}{\theta_{p}\left(q^{-2}t^{-2}a^{-2}\widetilde{c}_{4}^{-2}\right)\theta_{p}\left(\epsilon^{2}\right)\theta_{p}\left(q^{-1}\epsilon^{-2}\right)\theta_{p}\left(a^{-2}\widetilde{c}_{4}^{-1}c_{4}\epsilon^{2}\right)}\times

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\prod_{j=1}^{3}\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}\widetilde{c}_{4}\widetilde{c}_{j}\epsilon\right)\theta_{p}\left((pq)^{\frac{1}{2}}c_{4}c_{j}\epsilon\right)\mathcal{I}(\epsilon)+\{\epsilon\leftrightarrow\epsilon^{-1}\}\,.

(B.47)

Both of these operators above can be mapped onto the van Diejen model. Correspodning dictionaries can be read from the difference
parts of operators. In particular for operator (
B.46
) the map is given by:

\displaystyle h_{i}=ta^{-1}c_{i}^{-1}\,,\qquad h_{i+4}=ta\widetilde{c}_{i}^{-1}\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,4\,.

(B.48)

while for the operator (
B.47
) we obtain:

\displaystyle h_{i}=c_{4}^{-1}c_{i}^{-1}\,,\qquad h_{i+3}=\widetilde{c}_{4}^{-1}\widetilde{c}_{i}^{-1}\,,\quad i=1,2,3\,,\qquad h_{7,8}=t^{-2}a^{\pm 2}\,.

(B.49)

Just like in (
3.29
) van Diejen parameters map to the inverse
\mathrm{U}(1)
charges of the moment maps of the
puncture we act on as can be seen from (
3.25
). Constant parts of both operators (
B.46
)
and (
B.47
) are elliptic functions with periods
1
and
p
. It can be checked that poles and corresponding
residues of these constant parts coincide with those of van Diejen model summarized in (
3.6
) and (
3.7
).
Hence we conclude that both of these operators can differ from the van Diejen model at most by an irrelevant constant part.

B.5
Derivation of the
\mathrm{USp}(2N)
trinion

In this appendix we construct a three punctured sphere, with two maximal
\mathrm{USp}(2N)
punctures and one minimal
\mathrm{SU}(2)
puncture, of the minimal
(D_{N+3},D_{N+3})
conformal matter.
In the bulk of the paper we use only the degenerate case of
N=1
(
\mathrm{SU}(2)\sim USp(2)
) but here we will outline the more general construction.
The construction starts from the trinion
Razamat:2020bix
with two maximal
\mathrm{SU}(N+1)
punctures discussed in Section
3
,
and gluing to the two maximal
\mathrm{SU}(N+1)
punctures two tubes with one
\mathrm{SU}(N+1)
puncture and one
\mathrm{USp}(2N)
puncture
discussed in
Kim:2018bpg
(see Figure
17
). The dynamics of these gluings turns out to be S-confining.
The relevant S-confining gauge theory is depicted in Figure
18
. This duality
was discussed by Spiridonov and Vartanov in
Spiridonov:2009za
following mathematical results
of Spiridonov and Warnaar
MR2264067
and we will thus refer to it as Spiridonov-Warnaar-Vartanov
(SWV) duality. For
N=1,2
these dualities are special cases of the standard Seiberg duality.

Figure 17:
A tube theory with one
\mathrm{USp}(2N)
puncture and one
\mathrm{SU}(N+1)
puncture. The flux corresponding to this tube breaks the
6d

SO(4N+12)
symmetry to
U(1)\times SU(2N+6)
for general
N
(breaks
E_{8}
to
U(1)\times E_{7}
for
N=1
). The line starting and ending on the same gauge node is in two index antisymmetric representation. We have a superpotential terms corresponding to the triangle and coupling the two index antisymmetric to the antisymmetric square of the bifundamental between
\mathrm{USp}(2N)
and
\mathrm{SU}(N+1)
. The R charges depicted here are the six dimensional ones.

Let us briefly discuss the SWV duality. We start with asymptotically free
\mathrm{SU}(N+1)
SQCD with one chiral
field in the antisymmetric representation,
2N
fields in the anti-fundamental representation, and
N+3

fields in the fundamental representation. This is a non-anomalous vector like matter content. We have two
non-anomalous abelian symmetries: one depicted as
U(1)_{t}
in Figure
18
under which
the anti-fundamentals have charge
+1
and the antisymmetric field has charge
-2
, while under another one,
denoted by
U(1)_{b}
, the antifundamentals have charge
+\frac{1}{2N}
, the antisymmetric
charge
0
and the fundamentals charge
-\frac{1}{N+3}
. Anomaly free choice of R charges, which we denote
by
R_{6d}
, is depicted in Figure
18
. One can perform a-maximization
Intriligator:2003jj

on the two abelian symmetries and discover that the theory flows to an SCFT with all the operators above the unitarity
bound. For example for the case
N=2
the superconformal R-symmetry is,

\displaystyle R=R_{6d}+\frac{4}{5}q_{t}-\frac{8}{5}q_{b}\,.

(B.50)

Next we consider deforming the theory by a superpotential of the form
W=q^{2}A
. This is a relevant deformation for any
N
.
E.g.
for
N=2
the R-charge of this deformation is
\frac{6}{5}<2
.
This deformation breaks the
U(1)_{b}
symmetry. The claim of the SWV
Spiridonov:2009za
;
MR2264067
is that this theory flows to a WZ model in the IR, as depicted in Figure
18
.
In particular it was shown that the supersymmetric indices of the two dual sides are exactly the same. For our computations this equality is an essential input.

Figure 18:
Spiridonov-Warnaar-Vartanov (SWV) S-confining duality. The line starting and ending on the same gauge node is in two index antisymmetric representation. The theory on the left has a superpotential in the UV and the theory on the right has a dynamically generated superpotential. The duality can be reduced to Seiberg duality
Seiberg:1994rs
for low values of
N
. Note that for
N=1
the duality reduces to the S-confinement of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
with six fundamentals. For
N=2
we have
\mathrm{SU}(3)
SQCD with five flavors and a baryonic superpotential breaking
\mathrm{SU}(5)
to
\mathrm{USp}(4)
. The Seiberg dual theory is
\mathrm{SU}(2)
SQCD with five flavors, gauge singlets, and the effect of the baryonic superpotential is a mass term for four fundamentals. This leaves us again with S-confining
\mathrm{SU}(2)
with six fundamentals and a bunch of gauge singlet fields.

Let us now use the SWV duality to derive a three punctured sphere with two
\mathrm{USp}(2N)
maximal punctures.
For
N>1
the three punctured sphere we will derive will be an IR free gauge theory while for the
N=1

case it will be an interacting SCFT in the IR. We first give a derivation for general
N
and then give
more details for
N=1
. We start with the
\mathrm{SU}(N+1)
trinion and glue it to one of its
\mathrm{SU}(N+1)
maximal
punctures the tube. Note that we need to choose whether to
\Phi
glue, to S-glue, or a combination of
the two. Because of gauge anomalies we cannot use only
\Phi
gluing or S-gluing here. In fact the difference
between the number of components of the moment maps we use to glue in either way needs to be odd. We choose
to
\Phi
-glue all the components of the moment maps but one: the one we S-glue flips the baryonic moment
map component which is built from
N
of the quarks charged under the
\mathrm{SU}(N+1)
of the puncture we glue
and the
2
quarks charged under the minimal puncture
\mathrm{SU}(2)
. See Figure
19
.

Figure 19:
We glue a tube to one of the maximal
\mathrm{SU}(N+1)
punctures. The dotted line represents the moment map field coming
from the tube flipping the baryonic moment map of the trinion. We then use the SWV duality to reduce the dynamics of the

\mathrm{SU}(N+1)
gauge node to a WZ model and obtain the theory on the right.

In order to analyze the dynamics we turn on deformations one by one. We consider the three punctured
sphere with two
\mathrm{SU}(N+1)
punctures at the CFT point where all the chiral fields have superconformal
R-charge
+\frac{1}{2}
. First we turn on the gauge coupling. The anomaly
\textrm{Tr}RSU(N+1)^{2}=\frac{N+2}{4}
is
positive and thus the gauging is UV free. We flow to the IR and turn on the superpotentials one by one.
First, the superpotential flipping the baryonic moment map is irrelevant at this stage while the two
superpotential terms involving the bifundamental of
\mathrm{SU}(N+1)
and
\mathrm{USp}(2N)
are relevant. We first
turn on the one also involving the antisymmetric field. In the IR we perform a-maximization again and
now obtain that both remaining superpotentials are relevant. We then turn on the superpotential flipping
the baryonic moment map. The remaining superpotential is still relevant so we turn it on and flow to the IR.

Then we use the SWV duality in the version of Figure
20
to obtain a simpler theory
with only one
\mathrm{SU}(N+2)
gauge group. We thus obtain a trinion with one maximal
\mathrm{SU}(N+1)
puncture,
one maximal
\mathrm{USp}(2N)
puncture and one minimal
\mathrm{SU}(2)
puncture. The procedure is depicted in
Figure
19
. The theory on the right side of the Figure has two superpotential terms
coupling the antisymmetric field to the antisymmetrics of
\mathrm{SU}(N+2)
built from quadratic combinations
of the fields transforming under the puncture symmetries
\mathrm{SU}(2)
and
\mathrm{USp}(2N)
.
After performing the duality we have a UV free gauge theory with
\textrm{Tr}R_{free}SU(N+1)^{2}=\frac{2}{3}
, which is independent of
N
.

Next we can perform the same procedure on the remaining
\mathrm{SU}(N+1)
maximal puncture and we obtain a trinion with two maximal
\mathrm{USp}(2N)
punctures of Figure
21
.
The same analysis of the dynamics however gives different result. The gauging is UV free as before and then the two superpotentials
involving the bifundamental of
\mathrm{USp}(2N)
and
\mathrm{SU}(N+1)
are relevant while
the one involving the baryonic map is irrelevant. We can turn on the two relevant deformations one by one.
However, in the end for
N>1
the flipping of the baryonic moment map is still irrelevant deformation.
We can perform the SWV duality to see another vantage point of the issue. The theory after the duality is depicted in Figure
21

and for
N>1
this is an IR free gauge theory. Thus we obtain the trinion with two
\mathrm{USp}(2N)
maximal punctures and one
\mathrm{SU}(2)
puncture which is an
IR free gauge theory. We can still use the index of this model to deduce various properties of indices of general theories built from such
theories: in particular more complicated theories built from this might flow to interacting SCFTs in the IR. In this paper we explicitly discuss
only the interacting case of
N=1
and thus we will comment on it a bit more further in the text.

Figure 20:
A version of SWV duality with an additional field and a superpotential. We split
N+3
to
(N+2)+1
and add chiral field in fundamental of
\mathrm{USp}(2N)
which couples to the rest through a cubic superpotential corresponding to a triangle in the figure.

Let us now analyze the case of
N=1
. The S-confinement of Figure
18
is the Seiberg duality
between
\mathrm{SU}(2)
with six fundamentals and a WZ model of fifteen chiral fields. The antisymmetric field becomes
a gauge singlet field coupled to one of the quadratic invariants with a cubic superpotential and thus giving it
a mass in the IR. The trinion of Figure
21
for
N=1
is
\mathrm{SU}(3)
SQCD with eight flavors and
a baryonic superpotential. Note that the superconformal R-symmetry of all the chiral fields here is
\frac{5}{8}
.
Thus the baryonic superpotentials have R-charge
\frac{15}{8}
and are relevant. Turning these on one by one we arrive
at an SCFT. One can wonder what is the flux to be associated to this three punctured sphere. The theory manifestly
preserves
\mathrm{SU}(8)\times U(1)_{w}
and thus the flux should at least preserve this symmetry. There are two possible choices which
do that, an
E_{7}\times U(1)
or
\mathrm{SU}(8)\times U(1)
preserving fluxes
Kim:2017toz
. By
\Phi
gluing two three
punctured spheres into a genus two surface we can show that actually the latter one is the right answer. Let us first glue
two three punctured spheres into a four punctured sphere. To glue we need to add an octet of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
fundamental
fields, turn on cubic superpotentials and gauge the
\mathrm{SU}(2)
symmetry. The gauging is IR free before we turn on the
superpotentials. Thus we first take one trinion, add the octet of fields and turn on the cubic superpotential, which
is relevant. After turning on the superpotentials the gauging becomes exactly marginal. Note that
\textrm{Tr}\,RSU(2)^{2}=0
and
there are no anomalous symmetries. We can now glue all the other punctures following the same sequence of steps and
arrive at an SCFT. We can compute the index of the resulting theory and read the flux from it. In fact let us quote
the index of the theory obtained by following this procedure to construct a theory corresponding to a generic genus
g
surface,

\displaystyle{\cal I}=1+

(B.51)

\displaystyle\left(3g-3+({\bf 63}+{\bf 1})(g-1)+(g-1+3F){\bf 8}w^{-\frac{27}{2}}+(g-1-3F)\overline{\bf 8}w^{\frac{27}{2}}+(g-1-2F){\bf 28}w^{9}+\right.

\displaystyle\left.(g-1+2F)\overline{\bf 28}w^{-9}+(g-1+F){\bf 56}w^{-\frac{9}{2}}+(g-1-F)\overline{\bf 56}w^{\frac{9}{2}}\right)\,p\,q+\cdots\,.

with
F=g-1
.
As the decomposition of the adjoint of
E_{8}
into irreps of
\mathrm{SU}(8)\times U(1)_{w}
is,

\displaystyle{\bf 248}\to 1+{\bf 63}+{\bf 8}_{3}+\overline{\bf 8}_{-3}+{\bf 28}_{-2}+\overline{\bf 28}_{2}+{\bf 56}_{1}+\overline{\bf 56}_{-1}\,,

(B.52)

the above follows the expected pattern of
babuip
(see also Appendix E of
Kim:2017toz
). We can thus deduce that
to build a genus
g
surface we glue together
2g-2
three punctured spheres with each such sphere having half a unit of
\mathrm{SU}(8)\times U(1)

preserving flux associated to it. Yet another check is to compare the anomalies computed using this trinion versus the ones predicted from six dimensions.
For example the
a
anomaly for genus two surface obtained using the trinion here is
\frac{1}{16}\left(17\sqrt{17}+50\right)
which can be matched to
the
6d
value extracted
e.g.
from
Pasquetti:2019hxf
(
g=2
,
\xi_{G}=4
for
\mathrm{SU}(8)
flux, and
z=1
in equation (2.1) there).

Figure 21:
A trinion with two maximal
\mathrm{USp}(2N)
punctures and a minimal
\mathrm{SU}(2)
puncture. Note that the two maximal
punctures are of the same type (color). Moreover, following the logic of
Razamat:2019ukg
gluing together
N

such trinions, the
N
minimal punctures will recombine into an
\mathrm{USp}(2N)
maximal puncture somewhere on the conformal manifold.
Such a theory then will have tree maximal
\mathrm{USp}(2N)
punctures all of which are of the same color. For
N=1
this theory is
asymptotically free. For
N=2
the gauge and superpotential interactions are marginally irrelevant. The theory has a
U(1)

symmetry, such that the fundamentals have charge
+1
and the antisymmetrics charge
-5
, so that all the marginal couplings
have the same charge and thus there is no interacting conformal manifold
Green:2010da
;
Razamat:2020pra
. For higher values of
N
the gauge interactions are IR free.

B.6
Difference operator from
C_{1}
trinion.

In this Appendix we present some details of the derivations of van Diejen operator from
C_{1}
trinion summarized in the
Section
3
.
We start by S gluing the trinion (
3.30
) to a tube with no defect (
3.32
) along
y
puncture by gauging
corresponding
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{y}
global symmetry.
This gluing is S-confining,
i.e.
the resulting
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{y}
gauge node can be eliminated using
A_{1}
elliptic beta integral
(
A.4
). We end up with
\mathrm{SU}(3)\times\mathrm{SU}(2)
gauge theory. Then we perform Seiberg
duality on the
\mathrm{SU}(3)
node which has
7
flavors, so the duality results in
\mathrm{SU}(4)
gauge node with the same number of flavors.
Once we do that the
\mathrm{SU}(2)
node becomes
S-confining. Integrating it out we are left with
\mathrm{SU}(4)
gauge theory with
7
flavors, one antisymmetric and some flip singlets.
This chain of dualities is shown on the Figure
22
. Calculation results in the following index of a trinion theory:

Figure 22:
On this figure we show the chain of Seiberg dualities used in the derivation of the
C_{1}
trinion given in
(
B.53
). On the Figure (a) we start with the S gluing of the
C_{1}
trinion (
3.30
) with the tube (
3.32
)
obtained by closing
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{z}
minimal puncture with no defect. Then we use Seiberg duality on the S-confining
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{y}
node. Resulting
gauge theory is shown on the Figure (b). After this we perform two Seiberg dualities on one and another gauge nodes one after another. In the
end we arrive to the
\mathrm{SU}(4)
gauge theory with
7
flavors, one antisymmetric and some singlets. Corresponding quiver is shown on the
